People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Maybe a little, but I haven’t noticed it enough to say for sure.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any decrease in the pleasure you get from activities you usually enjoy?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 38 / waiter_or_waitress / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

Maybe I’m just not thinking about it much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it lost interest. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’ve been feeling pretty bad about myself. Like I messed up taking on this mortgage, and I’ve let my family down. Even little mistakes at work feel like proof I’m useless, like I deserve whatever comes from it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way about yourself?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. I’d say around a year, maybe a little over—about a year and two months that I’ve felt this way on and off.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the things you enjoy doing or how much pleasure you get from them over the past year?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really sure. I haven’t paid that much attention to it. I still do some of the same things, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you get as much pleasure from the things you used to enjoy as you did before you started feeling bad about yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m… not sure. I guess some things don’t feel the same, but I haven’t really thought about it that clearly.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you have less interest or pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Maybe a little, but I haven’t noticed it enough to say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any decrease in the pleasure you get from activities you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I can’t really say I’ve noticed a clear decrease. Some days things feel kind of flat, but… I don’t know if that’s really a change in enjoyment or just me not paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are things you used to enjoy that you no longer look forward to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really noticed anything specific like that. Maybe I’m just not thinking about it much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it lost interest. I mean… I still do things, I just haven’t really thought about whether I enjoy them the same or not.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been experiencing less pleasure than usual from activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way… things maybe feel a little dull sometimes, but I can’t say it’s less pleasure exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I guess. It’s not always like crying sad, just this heavy, empty feeling. I’m at work carrying plates and smiling because I’m supposed to, but I don’t really feel much good behind it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight over the past year?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really kept track of the number. My eating’s been kind of all over, though… some weeks I barely touch the staff meal, and other nights I get home and just eat whatever’s there. So maybe, but I’m not sure how much.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts about death or dying?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah… not like I’ve got a plan to hurt myself. But when I’m lying awake before an early shift, I’ll think maybe it’d be easier if I just didn’t wake up… didn’t have to keep messing everything up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling more restless than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah… some. People at work have mentioned it. Sometimes I’m pacing around checking the same tables or receipts again and again, and other times I’m just… slow. Like getting menus or walking over to the POS takes longer than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do. Mostly I feel like I’ve let my family down with the mortgage and everything. And at work, if I mess up even something small, it just… feels like proof I’m useless. Like I deserve whatever comes from it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself for your mistakes or perceived shortcomings?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Pretty often. It’s like every little mistake at work turns into proof that I’m useless, and with the mortgage I just keep thinking I’ve let my family down. I know that probably sounds harsh, but… that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself ruminating on past mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I keep going back over stuff, especially the mortgage and mistakes at work. Even little things start feeling like… I don’t know, proof that I’m useless or that I deserve things going bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you often put yourself down when you make a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. If I make a mistake at work, even a small one, I start telling myself I’m useless. And with the mortgage, it’s like… I keep thinking I’ve let my family down and maybe I deserve whatever happens.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
